This research was funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft
(DFG, German Research Foundation), project numbers 441423094, 236615297 - SFB 1119, the Austrian Science Fund (FWF) via the SFB BeyondC (10.55776/FG7) and an FWF START award, as well as the European Research Council (ERC) via the Starting grant q-shadows.

Appendix

The Appendix is organized as follows:
Appendix

A
briefly reviews the qudit-Clifford group, supplemental to the introduction of the Weyl-Heisenberg group in the main text. In
Appendix

B
we provide the proofs of some results which we claimed but did not prove in the main part, where other technical results are outsourced further to
Appendix

D
. Finally,
Appendix

E
features the quantum circuits necessary to run the
d
-copy-efficient protocol from
Section

4
.

Appendix A
Qudit quantum computing

Quantum computing in qubits makes heavily use of the Pauli matrices, which are ”classical” quantum operations, in the sense, that if one splits a qubit into a amplitude and a phase information, they merely act as the flip operator, they themselves can neither create nor destroy coherence in the computational basis. In the main text, we already provided the analogue of the Pauli group in higher dimensions, the Weyl-Heisenberg group. Similarly to the qubit case, we can then proceed to define the qudit-Clifford group as the symmetry group of the Weyl-Heisenberg matrices.

\mathcal{C}l=\{U|UWU^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{W}\text{ for all }W\in\mathcal{W}\}.

(19)

Similar to the qubit case, the Clifford group is the a maximal finite subgroup of the unitary group
\mathrm{U}(d)
. In particular, in the single-qudit case it has only two generators, which resemble the Hadamard and the S-gate. For the
n
-qudit Clifford group, next to the local generators
S
and
H
on each subsystem, on additionally needs
n-1
generators which generalise the role of the controlled-NOT operation in the multi-qudit case, see e.g.
[
38
]
. We now explicitly state these generators, and some of their properties inside the Clifford group.
First, consider the analogue of the Hadamard-gate, defined via

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:H-gate_{D}ef}H=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{k,j\in[d]}\omega^{kj}|k\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}j|.

(20)

It has order four
H^{4}=\mathds{1}
, and interchanges between the
X
and
Z
bases such that
HZH^{\dagger}=X
. The analogue to the
S
-gate in the qubit Clifford group, is the following:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:S-gate_{D}ef}S=\sum_{j\in[d]}\omega^{j(j-1)/2}|j\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}j|.

(21)

It satisfies
S^{d}=\mathds{1}
, and together with
H
generates
Z
via
Z=H^{2}S^{d-1}H^{2}S
. In particular, the operator
S
is constructed such that
|\psi^{a}_{j}\rangle=S^{a}|\psi^{0}_{j}\rangle
for
a\neq\infty
.
Additionally we have the following two-qudit gate, which serves as a generalization of the CNOT gate, where the addition on the second qudit is now simply performed modulo
d
:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:CX-gate_{D}ef}CX=\sum_{k,l\in[d]}|k\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}k|\otimes|k+l\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}l|.

(22)

Together, the local generators
S
and
H
on every qudit and the two-qudit gates
CX^{(j,j+1)}
for
j=1,\dots,n-1
generate the entire
n
-qudit Clifford group.

Appendix B
Outsourced Proofs from Main part

We begin with providing the proofs of the two main steps for deriving the lower bound on the sample complexity in
Section

3
.

Lemma B.1
.

[Compare with
[
21
]
, Thm. 15, slightly extended and modified vesrion thereof]

\delta_{c,\varepsilon}\leq\left(\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}\frac{(6\varepsilon)^{\left|S\right|}}{\sqrt{d(d-1)}^{n}}\sqrt{\max_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{2\left|S\right|}}\left\lVert\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}}W^{\otimes\tau}\right\rVert^{n}}\right)^{2}.

(23)

Proof.

The proof is essentially analogous to the one of Theorem 15 in
[
21
]
. Due to subtle differences in several places, however, we conduct the proof here nonetheless. First of all, we spell out the
\chi^{2}
-divergence for fixed
W
and measurement
M=\{M_{a}\}_{a\in A}
. Specifically, we have

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{M}\left((\rho_{W}^{\varepsilon})^{\otimes c}\left|\right|\rho_{m}^{\otimes c}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\tr(M_{a}\rho_{m}^{\otimes c})\left(\frac{\tr(M_{a}(\rho_{W}^{\varepsilon})^{\otimes c})}{\tr(M_{a}\rho_{m}^{\otimes c}}-1\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\frac{\tr(M_{a})}{d^{cn}}\left(\frac{\tr(M_{a}(\mathbbm{1}+3\varepsilon(W+W^{\dagger}))^{\otimes c})}{\tr(M_{a})}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\frac{1}{\tr(M_{a})d^{cn}}\left(\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}(3\varepsilon)^{\left|S\right|}\tr(M_{a}(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S}\right)^{2}.

Next, we introduce arbitrary non-zero numbers
w_{S}>0
for each
0\neq S\in[2]^{c}
and apply Cauchy Schwarz in a particular way, to find that for any probability distribution
\mu
:

\displaystyle\max_{M}\mathbb{E}_{W\sim\mu}\left[\sum_{a\in A}\frac{1}{\tr(M_{a})d^{cn}}\left(\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}\frac{\sqrt{w_{S}}}{\sqrt{w_{S}}}(3\varepsilon)^{\left|S\right|}\tr(M_{a}(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{M}\mathbb{E}_{W\sim\mu}\left[\sum_{a\in A}\frac{1}{\tr(M_{a})d^{cn}}\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}\frac{1}{w_{S}}(3\varepsilon)^{2\left|S\right|}\tr[M_{a}(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S}]\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}w_{S}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}w_{S}\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}\frac{(3\varepsilon)^{2\left|S\right|}}{w_{S}}\max_{M}\mathbb{E}_{W\sim\mu}\left[\sum_{a}\frac{\tr(M_{a}(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S})}{d^{cn}\tr(M_{a})}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}\sqrt{\max_{M}\mathbb{E}_{W\sim\mu}\left[\sum_{a}\frac{\tr(M_{a}(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S})}{d^{cn}\tr[M_{a}]}\right]}\right)^{2}.

where we picked
w_{S}=\sqrt{\max_{M}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\sum_{a}\frac{\tr(M_{a}(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S})}{d^{cn}\tr[M_{a}]}}>0
to arrive at the last term.
For any distribution
\mu
and (finite)
POVM
(
POVM
)
M=\{M_{a}\}_{a\in A}
we can upper-bound

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:auxiliary_{b}ound}\delta_{c,\varepsilon}\leq\left(\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}(3\varepsilon)^{\left|S\right|}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{W\sim\mu}\left[\sum_{a\in A}\frac{\tr[M_{a}(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S}]^{2}}{d^{cn}\tr[M_{a}]}\right]}\right)^{2}.

(24)

Let us now pick the distribution
\hat{\mu}
specified before and let
\{M_{a}\}_{a\in A}
be an arbirtrary POVM acting on
\mathbb{C}^{dn}
. In order to expand the tensor product
(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S}
we introduce the following index set: For a fixed
S\in[2]^{c}
, let
\mathcal{I}(S)=\{I\in\{-1,0,1\}^{c}|\supp(S)=\supp(I)\}
. Note that
\left|\mathcal{I}(S)\right|=2^{\left|S\right|}
, where
\left|S\right|
here refers to the number of non-zero entries of
S
. Due to unitarity, it then holds that
(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S}=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}(S)}W^{\otimes\tau}
. Via simple algebraic manipulations, and the fact that for any operators
A,B
where
A
is positive semidefinite, it holds that
\left|\tr[AB]\right|\leq\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\infty}\tr[A]
, we can derive an upper bound for the term inside the square root in
Eq.

24
:

\displaystyle\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{n}}\sum_{a\in A}\frac{\tr[M_{a}(W+W^{\dagger})^{\otimes S}]^{2}}{d^{cn}\tr[M_{a}]}=\sum_{a\in A}\frac{1}{d^{cn}\tr[M_{a}]}\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{n}}\tr\left[M_{a}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}(S)}W^{\otimes\tau}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A}\frac{1}{d^{cn}\tr[M_{a}]}\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{n}}\tr\left[M_{a}\otimes M_{a}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}(S)}W^{\otimes\tau}\otimes\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}(S)}W^{\otimes\tau}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A}\frac{1}{d^{cn}\tr[M_{a}]}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}(S)^{2}}\tr\left[M_{a}\otimes M_{a}\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{n}}W^{\otimes\tau}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A}\frac{\tr[M_{a}\otimes M_{a}]}{d^{cn}\tr[M_{a}]}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}(S)^{2}}\left\lVert\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{n}}W^{\otimes\tau}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}(S)^{2}}\left\lVert\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{n}}W^{\otimes\tau}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}(S)^{2}}\left\lVert\left(\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}}W^{\otimes\tau}\right)^{\otimes n}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{I}(S)^{2}}\left\lVert\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{n}}W^{\otimes\tau}\right\rVert^{n}_{\infty}=4^{\left|S\right|}\max_{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{2\left|S\right|}}\left\lVert\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{n}}W^{\otimes\tau}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{n}.

Clearly, for sites
k
where
\tau_{k}=0
we have
W^{\tau(k)}=\mathbbm{1}
on this sites for all
W
. Therefore the sites can be factored into a tensor product of the identity with the sum over the remaining sites. Since tensoring with the identity does not change the operator norm of any operator, we can therefore assume that the strings
\tau
only take values in
\{\pm 1\}
and are of length
\left|S\right|
. However, since we are in a tensor can be can be pulled out of the summation. To summarize, since
\left|\hat{\mathcal{W}}^{n}\right|=(d(d-1))^{n}
we end up with the estimate

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:reduction_{t}o_{t}wirls}\delta_{\varepsilon,c}\leq\left(\sum_{0\neq S\in[2]^{c}}\frac{(6\varepsilon)^{2\left|S\right|}}{\sqrt{d(d-1)}^{n}}\sqrt{\max{\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{2\left|S\right|}}\left\lVert\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}}W^{\otimes\tau}\right\rVert_{\infty}}\right)^{2}

(25)

∎

We state the following theorem slightly more general than in the main text, in order to also apply it to the later extensions.
For convenience of the reader, we repeat the statement of
Proposition

8
before giving its proof. In particularly, we state it slightly more general than in the main text, to highlight its applicability to the situation in
Section

5.1
.

Proposition
8

(restated)
.

Let
d\geq 3
be a prime,
m
a natural number, and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{2m}
. Then we have

\left\lVert\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}}W^{\otimes\tau}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\begin{cases}d(d-1)&\text{ for }m=0\mod d,\\
d&\text{ otherwise}.\par\end{cases}

(26)

Proof.

In order to avoid confusion from the start, all occurences of
\delta
in this proof refer to the Kronecker delta, not to
Eq.

6
. Also, for simplicity we assume
m\leq d
, since only the parity of
m
modulo
d
is important here.
For the purpose of our proof let
M_{\tau}:=\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}}W^{\otimes\tau}
. In order to keep the presentation of the proof as neat as possible, let
\kappa:=\langle\tau,\mathbf{1}\rangle/2\in\mathbb{Z}
and
T(J):=\langle\tau,J\rangle
for
J\in[d]^{2m}
. As we will show in the following, the respective exact expressions of
M_{\tau}
are given by

\displaystyle M_{\tau}

\displaystyle=d\sum_{T(J)\neq\kappa}|J+m^{-1}(\kappa-T(J))\tau\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|

\displaystyle\text{for }m<d,

(27)

\displaystyle M_{\tau}

\displaystyle=d\sum_{T(J)=\kappa}\sum_{a=1}^{d-1}|J+a\tau\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|

\displaystyle\text{for }m=d.

(28)

From these expression it is not hard to derive the claim of the theorem. To prove that
\left\lVert M_{\tau}\right\rVert=d
in the case
m<d
, it suffices to show that the mapping
J\mapsto J+m^{-1}(\kappa-T(J))\tau
is injective: Let
J,K
such that

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:injectivity}J+m^{-1}(\kappa-T(J))\tau=K+m^{-1}(\kappa-T(K))\tau.

(29)

By applying
T(\cdot)
to this condition we find that
T(J)=T(K)
must hold, since
T(\tau)=\langle\tau,\tau\rangle=2m
. Clearly, inserting this relation back into
Eq.

29
it then also follows that
J=K
.

In the latter case
m=d
, first note, that
M_{\tau}
is the sum (over
a
) of
d-1
operators, each with operator norm
d
. This directly leads to the upper bound
\left\lVert M_{\tau}\right\rVert\leq d(d-1)
. On the other hand, consider the (unnormalized) state
|\psi\rangle=\sum_{a=0}^{d-1}|2^{-1}\mathbf{1}+a\tau\rangle
. Since
T(\tau)=_{d}0
,
|\psi\rangle
is contained in the subspace spanned by the
|J\rangle
with
T(J)=\kappa
. It is easy checked that this is an eigenstate of
M_{\tau}
:

M_{\tau}|\psi\rangle=d\sum_{a=1}^{d-1}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}|2^{-1}\mathbf{1}+(a+b)\tau\rangle=d(d-1)|\psi\rangle.

In particular, this shows
\left\lVert M_{\tau}\right\rVert\geq d(d-1)
, so equality must hold.
Let us now derive the expressions
Eq.

27
,
Eq.

28
.
First of all, we may assume without loss of generality that
\tau_{1}=1
, since exchanging
W\leftrightarrow W^{-1}=W^{\dagger}
in the summation gives the same result. We split the sum into summations the subgroups
\mathcal{W}_{a}=\langle W_{a}\rangle
for
a=0,\dots,d-1
, (which only intersect at the identity) and express the operators in terms of their eigenprojectors.

\displaystyle\sum_{W\in\hat{\mathcal{W}}}W^{\otimes\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in[d]}\sum_{k\in[d]}(W_{\alpha}^{k})^{\otimes\tau}-d\mathds{1}=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{k}\sum_{J\in[d]^{2m}}\omega^{k\langle\tau,J\rangle}\psi^{\alpha}_{J}-d\mathds{1}

\displaystyle=d\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{\langle\tau,J\rangle=0}\psi^{\alpha}_{J}-d\mathds{1}.

For the next step, we exploit the fact that the
\omega^{j}
-eigenstates of the different
W_{a}
are related to each other via the action of the
S
-gate, and spell out everything in the computational basis. Also, we abbreviate
f(K)=\sum_{i}k_{i}(k_{i}-1)=\langle K,K-\mathbf{1}\rangle
, which is always an even number.

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{T(J)=0}\psi^{\alpha}_{J}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{T(J)}(S^{\alpha})^{\otimes 2m}\psi^{0}_{J}(S^{-\alpha})^{\otimes 2m}

\displaystyle=d^{-2m}\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{T(J)=0}\sum_{K,L}(S^{\alpha})^{\otimes 2m}\omega^{-\langle{J,K}\rangle}\omega^{+\langle J,L\rangle}|K\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}L|(S^{-\alpha})^{\otimes 2m}

\displaystyle=d^{-1}\sum_{\alpha}(S^{\alpha})^{\otimes m}\sum_{K,L}\delta_{(l_{1}-k_{1})\tau=L-K}|K\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}L|(S^{-\alpha})^{\otimes 2m}

\displaystyle=d^{-1}\sum_{\alpha,K,L}\delta_{\Delta\tau=K-L}\omega^{\alpha(f(K)-f(L))/2}|K\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}L|

\displaystyle=\sum_{K,L}\delta_{\Delta\tau=K-L}\delta_{f(K)=f(L)}|K\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}L|.

In the third line we applied
D.2
, and in the fourth line we substituted
\Delta:=k_{1}-l_{1}
. Now we need to jointly solve these two equations. For this purpose, let
L
be fixed, and we determine all solutions
\Delta
.
We substitute the linear constraint
K=L+\Delta\tau
into the quadratic equation
f(K)=f(L)
and obtain the following condition:

\displaystyle f(L+\Delta\tau)

\displaystyle=\langle L+\Delta\tau,L+\Delta\tau\rangle-\langle L+\Delta\tau,\mathbf{1}\rangle=\langle L,L\rangle-\langle L,\mathbf{1}\rangle=f(L),

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;2\Delta\langle\tau,L\rangle+\Delta^{2}\langle\tau,\tau\rangle-\Delta\langle\tau,\mathbf{1}\rangle=0,

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;2\Delta T(L)+2m\Delta^{2}-\Delta 2\kappa=0.

Since
d
is prime
\geq 3
, this is equivalent to the following quadratic equation which is central to the proof:

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:quadtratic_{e}quation}\left(m\Delta+T(L)-\kappa\right)\Delta=0.

(30)

So far, all calculations worked independently of
m
. However, to analyze the solutions of
Eq.

30
we need to distinguish two fundamentally different cases.
When
m
is divisible by
d
, the quadratic part of the equation vanishes, and for
T(L)-\kappa\neq 0
we are left with the only solution
\Delta=0
. However, for
T(L)=\kappa
, all
\Delta=0,\dots,d-1
are solutions. Hence, we find

\displaystyle M_{\tau}

\displaystyle=d\left(\sum_{T(J)\neq\kappa}|J\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|+\sum_{T(J)=\kappa}\sum_{a\in[d]}|J+a\tau\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|-\mathds{1}\right)

\displaystyle=d\left(-\sum_{T(J)=\kappa}|J\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|+\sum_{T(J)=\kappa}\sum_{a\in[d]}|J+a\tau\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|\right)=d\sum_{T(J)=\kappa}\sum_{a=1}^{d-1}|J+a\tau\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|.

On the other hand, when
d<m
,
Eq.

30
has only the two solutions
\Delta=0
and
\Delta=m^{-1}(\kappa-T(L))
which of course coincide when
T(L)=\kappa
. Therefore we find

\displaystyle M_{\tau}

\displaystyle=d\left(\sum_{J}|J\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|+\sum_{T(J)\neq\kappa}|J+m^{-1}(\kappa-T(J))\tau\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|-\mathds{1}\right)

(31)

\displaystyle=d\sum_{T(J)\neq\kappa}|J+m^{-1}(\kappa-T(J))\tau\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}J|.

(32)

This concludes the proof.
∎

Note, that the result above covers only the situation where
d
is an odd prime, and we are averaging over the reduced Weyl-Heisenberg group
\hat{\mathcal{W}}_{d}
. To obtain our result in
Section

5.1
we also need a result for the even prime
d=2
, which we state here.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
k
be an integer and
\mathcal{P}=\{I,X,Y,Z\}
the set of the four Pauli matrices. Then:

\left\lVert\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P^{\otimes 2k}\right\rVert_{\infty}=3+(-1)^{k}.

(33)

Proof.

This is a straightforward calculation: We substitute
X=|0\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}1|+|1\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}0|
,
Y=i|0\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}1|-i|1\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}0|
, and
I,Z
analogously. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in\{I,X,Y,Z\}}P^{\otimes 2k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in[2]^{2k}}|I\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}I|+(-1)^{\left|I\right|}|I\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}I|+|I\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}I\oplus\mathbf{1}|+i^{2k}(-1)^{\left|I\right|}|I\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}I\oplus\mathbf{1}|

(34)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in[2]^{2k}}(1+(-1)^{\left|I\right|})|I\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}I|+(1+(-1)^{k+\left|I\right|})|I\kern 0.6458pt\rangle\langle\kern 0.6458pt\mathopen{}I\oplus\mathbf{1}|.

(35)

In particular, when
k
is odd, for every
I\in[2]^{k}
either of the two factors will vanish, leading to
\left\lVert\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P^{\otimes 2k}\right\rVert_{\infty}=2
. On the other hand, when
k
is even, with
|GHZ_{n}\rangle
being the GHZ-state on
n
qubits, we clearly find
\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}}P^{\otimes 2k}|GHZ_{2k}\rangle=4|GHZ_{2k}\rangle
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, to conclude this section, we provide the proof that the mimicking state algorithm
Algorithm

1
works correctly.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\rho
a fixed quantum states, and
u_{W}
estimates of
\left|\tr(W\rho)\right|
up to additive error
\varepsilon/6
. Then with probability
p\geq 0.99
the output of
Algorithm

1
provides a classical description of a state
\sigma
such that
\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon,\rho}\subset\mathcal{W}_{\varepsilon/3,\sigma}
.

Proof.

First of all, the algorithm is guaranteed to be correct (with high probability) whenever no violating
W\in\mathcal{W}^{n}
is found in step 1.

Let us therefore assume that the algorithm iterates over all
t=0,\dots,T
and reaches the last step
t=T
. Let
y_{t}=\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\sigma_{t})
for
0\leq t\leq T
. By Lemma 3.1 from
[
29
]
we are guaranteed that after
T
steps of the algorithm:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sgn(y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t})\cdot y_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\min}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sgn(y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t})\cdot\hat{W}_{t}\right)+2\sqrt{nT}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sgn(y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t})\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\rho)+2\sqrt{nT}.

This can be equivalently expressed as

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:algorithm_{k}ey_{e}stimate}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sgn(y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t})\left(y_{t}-\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\rho)\right)\leq 2\sqrt{nT}.

(36)

Next, observe, that we can lower bound the difference

\displaystyle\left|\tr(W_{t}\sigma_{t})-z_{t}\right|\geq\left|\left|\tr(W_{t}\sigma_{t})\right|-u_{t}\right|-\left|\left|z_{t}\right|-u_{t}\right|\geq\varepsilon/2-(\varepsilon/8+\varepsilon/30)>\varepsilon/3.

This in turn leads to the following estimates:

\displaystyle\left|y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t}\right|=\left|\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\sigma_{t})-\hat{z}_{t}\right|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left|\tr(W_{t}\sigma_{t})-z_{t}\right|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3\sqrt{2}},

(37)

\displaystyle\left|y_{t}-\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\rho)\right|\geq\left|y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t}\right|-\left|\hat{z}_{t}-\tr[\hat{W}\rho]\right|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3\sqrt{2}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{30}>\frac{\varepsilon}{5}.

(38)

The first estimate, together with
\left|z_{t}-\tr(W_{t}\rho)\right|\leq\varepsilon/30
can be used to show that
\sgn(y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t})=\sgn(y_{t}-\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\rho))
. This can be easily shown by distinguishing the following two cases:

\displaystyle\text{If }\;y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3\sqrt{2}}\;\Rightarrow\;y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t}-(\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\rho)-\hat{z}_{t})\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3\sqrt{2}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{30}>0,

\displaystyle\text{If }\;\hat{z}_{t}-y_{t}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3\sqrt{2}}\;\Rightarrow\;\hat{z}_{t}-y_{t}-(\hat{z}_{t}-\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\rho))\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{3\sqrt{2}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{30}>0.

We use this fact, together with
Eq.

38
to lower bound the left hand side of
Eq.

36
:

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{nT}\geq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sgn(y_{t}-\hat{z}_{t})\left(y_{t}-\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\rho)\right)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left|y_{t}-\tr(\hat{W}_{t}\rho)\right|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{5}T,

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;\;

\displaystyle T\leq 100n/\varepsilon^{2}.

This however is a contradiction to the way
T
was chosen beforehand, i.e. the algorithm never reaches the last step.
Let us finally estimate the probability that this algorithm fails. The only place where it can lead to erroneous results is when estimating the
z_{t}
in stap 3 a). The proability that all of these estimates are correct is
p\geq(1-0.01/\left|T\right|)^{\left|T\right|}\geq 1-0.01
. Hence it follows, that the algorithm outputs a correct mimicking state
\sigma
in
99
out of
100
runs.
∎

Appendix C
Remarks on Numerical Simulations

To quantify the sampling requirements of the proposed certification protocol, we performed numerical simulations for systems of up to
10
qutrits. For each system size, we determine the minimal number of samples
N_{\min}
that guarantees a success probability
p_{\mathrm{succ}}\geq 0.7
.
The success probability measures how often the protocol correctly identifies all tested expectation values
\operatorname{tr}(W\rho)
within some tolerance.

C.1
Wilson confidence strategy

To decide whether a given number of samples is sufficient, we employ the Wilson confidence-interval strategy for binomial success probabilities. Given
t
repetitions of the reconstruction experiment at fixed sample size
N
with
s
observed successes, the estimated probability is
\hat{p}=s/t
. The Wilson interval
[
39
]
provides an adjusted confidence bound that remains reliable even for small
t
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[p\in(w_{+},w_{-})\right]=1-\alpha\quad\text{with }w_{\pm}=\frac{n\hat{p}+\frac{z^{2}}{2}}{t+z^{2}}\pm\frac{z\sqrt{t}}{t+z^{2}}\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})+\frac{z^{2}}{4t}},

(39)

where
z
is the standard normal quantile corresponding to the desired confidence level
1-\alpha=90\%
.
The lower bound of this interval serves as a conservative estimate of the true success probability.
For a target success probability
p_{\star}=0.7
we certify that the true success rate at sample size
N
satisfies
p_{N}\geq p_{\star}
whenever
w_{-}\geq p_{\star}
.
At each fixed
N
we apply the following stopping rule:

\displaystyle\text{Decision}=\begin{cases}\textbf{Accept},&\text{if }w_{-}\geq p_{\star},\\[4.0pt]
\textbf{Early-reject},&\text{if }w_{+}<p_{\star},\\[4.0pt]
\text{Continue sampling (increase $t$) up to }t_{\max},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(40)

If
t
reaches
t_{\max}
while
w_{-}<p_{\star}\leq w_{+}
(undecided), we conservatively treat this
N
as a reject.

Across sample sizes, we perform an exponential search: starting at
N_{0}
, increase
N\leftarrow\max\{N+1,\,\lfloor gN\rfloor\}
(with growth factor
g>1
) whenever the decision at the current
N
is reject or undecided, until we encounter the first accepted
N
(call it
N_{\mathrm{hit}}
). If a last failing point
N_{\mathrm{fail}}
is known, we optionally refine via bisection on the integer interval
(N_{\mathrm{fail}},\,N_{\mathrm{hit}}]
using the same Wilson decision at each midpoint, returning the minimal accepted
N
. This value is reported as
N_{\min}
.

C.2
Three-copy strategies

In the three-copy setting we estimate, for an
n
-qutrit state
\rho
, the expectations
\langle W\rangle=\mathrm{tr}(W\rho)
of Weyl strings
W\in\mathcal{W}^{\otimes n}
, where
\mathcal{W}
denotes the single-qutrit Weyl–Heisenberg set (of size
|\mathcal{W}|=d^{2}=9
). All
|\mathcal{W}|^{\,n}=9^{\,n}
strings are verified simultaneously from the same 3-copy data.
A reconstruction experiment counts as success if for every
W\in\mathcal{W}^{\otimes n}
the reconstructed value
\hat{y}
sufficiently matches the theoretical target. That is
\max_{W}|\tr(W^{\otimes 3}\rho^{\otimes 3})-\hat{y}|<\delta
where we used
\delta=0.1
in our experiments. At fixed sample size
N
, the accept/reject decision uses the Wilson rule of Sec.
C.1
to certify
p_{\mathrm{succ}}\geq 0.7
; the minimal accepted
N
is reported as
N_{\min}
.

C.3
Single-copy strategies

In the single-copy baseline we fix a hidden Weyl string
W^{\star}\in\mathcal{W}_{n}
(which determines
\rho
) and draw measurement strings
W
uniformly at random from
\mathcal{W}_{n}\;=\;\{\,X^{a_{1}}Z^{b_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes X^{a_{n}}Z^{b_{n}}\;:\;a_{j},b_{j}\in\{1,2\}\,\}
,
so that
|\mathcal{W}_{n}|=4^{n}
(each site uses one of the four non-identity pairs
(a,b)\in\{1,2\}^{2}
). A draw is a success iff
W=W^{\star}
or
W={W^{\star}}^{\dagger}
. Hence, a single draw has hit probability
p_{\text{hit}}=2/4^{-n}
and, with
N
independent draws, the probability of at least one hit is:

\displaystyle p(N)=1-(1-2/4^{-n})^{N}.

(41)

For each fixed
N
, we repeat
t=2000
times and record the number of successful retrievals. The empirical success probability is
\hat{p}(N)=s/t
. We iterate over a fine grid of
N
and report

\displaystyle N_{\min}^{\mathrm{emp}}=\min\{N:\hat{p}(N)\geq 0.7\}.

(42)

C.4
Simulation summary

Figure
1
summarizes the scaling of the minimal sample number
N_{\min}
with the number of qutrits. The five curves correspond to the strategies described above: three-copy full check, three-copy random check (Wilson), three-copy random check (empirical), one-copy empirical, and one-copy theoretical. The data show that the three-copy protocol dramatically reduces the sampling complexity compared to the single-copy baseline, to achieve the prescribed success probability.

Appendix D
Technical Lemmata

Lemma D.1

(Hoeffding)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
be independent real-valued random variables, not necessarily identically distributed, such that
a_{i}\leq X_{i}\leq b_{i}
for suitable
a_{i},b_{i}
almost surely. Then, for any
c\geq 0
it holds that

\Pr\left[\frac{1}{N}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}-\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]\right|\geq c\right]\leq 2\exp\left(\frac{2c^{2}N^{2}}{\sum_{i}(b_{i}-a_{i})^{2}}\right).

(43)

Lemma D.2
.

Let
d
be a prime,
\omega
a primitive
d-th
root of unity, and
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\neq 0
be vectors on
\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{m}
and assume that
a_{1}=1
. Then

\sum_{\langle\mathbf{a},I\rangle=0}\omega^{\langle\mathbf{b},I\rangle}=d^{m-1}\delta_{b_{1}\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{b}}.

(44)

Proof.

First of all, the condition
\langle a,I\rangle=0
translates to
i_{1}=-\sum_{k\geq 2}i_{k}a_{k}
. Therefore, we express the summation as

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{2},\dots,i_{m}}\omega^{-b_{1}\sum_{k\geq 2}a_{k}i_{k}}\prod_{k\geq 2}\omega^{b_{k}i_{k}}=\sum_{i_{2},\dots,i_{m}}\prod_{k\geq 2}\omega^{i_{k}(b_{k}-b_{1}a_{k})}=d^{m-1}\prod_{k\geq 2}\delta_{b_{k}=b_{1}a}=d^{m-1}\delta_{b_{1}\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{b}},

where we used that the condition
b_{1}a_{1}=b_{1}
is trivially satisfied by construction in the last step.
∎

Appendix E
Quantum Circuits

In this section we provide examples of quantum circuits to implement the measurement processes in the main part.

E.1
Quantum circuits for generalized Bell-basis measurements

In order to show, that the quantum circuits required to execute the generalized Bell-basis measurements are efficient, we sketch their structure, which strongly resembles the circuits for the preparation of ordinary Bell states in the circuits below. Specifically, for
n=1
we have that the diagonalizing unitary can be decomposed into single and two-qubit qudit Cliffords as

C=H^{(1)}\circ CX^{d-1,d}\circ CX^{d-2,d-1}\circ\dots\circ CX^{1,2}.

(45)

For general
n
, the diagonalizing unitary is then simply
C^{\otimes n}
, which is schematically depicted in the circuit below for the example of qu
trits
.

and
H_{3}
the Hadamard/QFT as in
Eq.

20
.

E.2
Transpilation to qubit architectures for the special case
d=3

Clearly, in order to faithfully represent qutrit gates, it suffices to embed one qutrit into two qubits. Here, we have to ensure, that the representation is reducible (into a trivial one-dimensional component and a three-dimensional irreducible one). Specifically, we choose the embedding via the binary representation, i.e.
|0\rangle\mapsto|00\rangle,|1\rangle\mapsto|01\rangle,|2\rangle\mapsto|10\rangle
. First, we consider the qutrit Hadamard-gate
H_{3}
. The eigenvalues of the Blockmatrix
\begin{pmatrix}H_{3}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

are given by
\{1,i,-1,1\}
with the corresponding (unnormalized) eigenvectors
\{(1-\sqrt{3},1,1,0),(0,1,-1,0),(1+\sqrt{3},1,1,0),(0,0,0,1)\}
. Alltogether, we can therefore can express it as

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:H_{c}ircuit}\hbox to100.3pt{\vbox to28.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 50.14893pt\lower-14.17828pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

-k=2k
, and we can realize the controlled-X operation conveniently as follows:
